{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_018E4H5eMdyzhmjGQm5D2UmA","type":"tool_result","content":"\"\"\"Named real-world values, with provenance.\n\nThe bottleneck on a one-off part is not geometry, it is dimensions. An agent cannot\nmeasure a freezer shelf, and a guessed number produces a perfect model of the wrong\nobject: it builds, it checks clean, and it prints. Nothing downstream catches it.\n\nSo a measurement is a named value on disk with a note saying how it was obtained, and\nasking for one that is not on file raises. That failure is the feature. It is the moment\nto ask rather than to pick something plausible.\n\"\"\"\n\nimport pathlib\nimport sys\nimport tomllib\n\nFILENAME = \"measurements.toml\"\n\n\nclass MeasurementError(LookupError):\n    \"\"\"A measurement is missing or unusable.\n\n    Not `KeyError`, which reprs its argument: a message with a worked example in it comes\n    out as one quoted string full of backslash-n, and the example is the useful part.\n    \"\"\"\n\n\ndef _caller_dir(depth):\n    \"\"\"Where the code that called us lives, so the lookup needs no configuration.\"\"\"\n    frame = sys._getframe(depth)\n    here = frame.f_globals.get(\"__file__\")\n    return pathlib.Path(here).resolve().parent if here else pathlib.Path.cwd()\n\n\ndef _find(start):\n    \"\"\"The nearest measurements file, without leaving the project.\n\n    The walk stops at the project root, meaning the directory holding `parts/`. Without\n    that stop it would keep going to the filesystem root and could silently answer with\n    some unrelated file from an ancestor directory, which is the exact failure this whole\n    module exists to prevent: a dimension that is wrong and looks fine.\n    \"\"\"\n    for d in [start, *start.parents]:\n        if (d / FILENAME).is_file():\n            return d / FILENAME\n        if (d / \"parts\").is_dir():\n            return None\n    return None\n\n\ndef _read(path):\n    \"\"\"Parse it, and say which file when it will not parse. The same courtesy cards get.\"\"\"\n    try:\n        return tomllib.loads(path.read_text(encoding=\"utf-8\"))\n    except tomllib.TOMLDecodeError as exc:\n        raise MeasurementError(f\"{path.name}: not valid TOML ({exc})\") from exc\n\n\ndef load(start=None):\n    \"\"\"Every measurement on file, as name -> record. Empty when there is no file.\"\"\"\n    path = _find(pathlib.Path(start).resolve() if start else _caller_dir(2))\n    return _read(path) if path else {}\n\n\ndef provisional(start=None):\n    \"\"\"Every value on file that admits it was not actually measured.\n\n    The doctrine says to ask rather than improvise, and sometimes there is nobody to\n    ask: the caliper is in a drawer, the object is at the office, the print has to go\n    on tonight. The failure mode is not putting a number in, it is that the number then\n    looks exactly like one somebody measured. Six months later nothing distinguishes\n    them and the part is wrong for a reason no one can find.\n\n    So a guess is allowed to be written down and required to say so, and `nurb check`\n    reports the ones that still are. `how` stays mandatory either way: a provisional\n    value still has to say where it came from, because \"eyeballed against a broom\"\n    tells the next person how much to trust it.\n    \"\"\"\n    return sorted(\n        (name, entry.get(\"how\", \"\"))\n        for name, entry in load(start).items()\n        if entry.get(\"provisional\")\n    )\n\n\ndef measured(name, start=None):\n    \"\"\"A measured value, in mm.\n\n    Raises when it is not on file, when it has no provenance, or when it is recorded in\n    units this does not work in. All three are cases where continuing would produce\n    geometry that looks right and is wrong.\n\n    A value marked `provisional = true` is returned like any other. It is a real number\n    that builds a real part; what it is not is measured, and `nurb check` says so until\n    somebody picks up a caliper.\n    \"\"\"\n    start = pathlib.Path(start).resolve() if start else _caller_dir(2)\n    path = _find(start)\n    if path is None:\n        raise MeasurementError(\n            f\"no {FILENAME} above {start}, so there is nothing measured to ask for. \"\n            f\"Measure {name!r}, then record it:\\n\\n\"\n            f'  [{name}]\\n  value = 0\\n  unit = \"mm\"\\n  how = \"how you measured it\"\\n\\n'\n            f\"If there is nobody to ask right now, add `provisional = true` and put \"\n            f\"your best guess in. `nurb check` will keep reminding you.\"\n        )\n    book = _read(path)\n    if name not in book:\n        have = \", \".join(sorted(book)) or \"nothing yet\"\n        raise MeasurementError(\n            f\"nothing measured called {name!r} in {path.name}. have: {have}. \"\n            f\"Ask for the measurement rather than guessing one: a wrong number here \"\n            f\"builds, checks clean, and prints.\"\n        )\n    entry = book[name]\n    if \"value\" not in entry:\n        raise MeasurementError(f\"{path.name}: {name} has no value\")\n    if \"how\" not in entry:\n        raise MeasurementError(\n            f\"{path.name}: {name} does not say how it was measured. A value without \"\n            f\"provenance is a guess with a filename on it.\"\n        )\n    unit = entry.get(\"unit\", \"mm\")\n    if unit != \"mm\":\n        raise MeasurementError(f\"{path.name}: {name} is in {unit!r}, and nurb works in mm\")\n    return entry[\"value\"]","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"96cf8d31-4e94-4d55-a648-73c13c46b367","uuid":"c17f5729-0d46-4174-85af-e4a23382057f","timestamp":"2026-08-24T23:32:55.816Z","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"\"\"\"Named real-world values, with provenance.\n\nThe bottleneck on a one-off part is not geometry, it is dimensions. Without\n    that stop it would keep going to the filesystem root and could silently answer with\n    some unrelated file from an ancestor directory, which is the exact failure this whole\n    module exists to prevent: a dimension that is wrong and looks fine.\n    \"\"\"\n    for d in [start, *start.parents]:\n        if (d / FILENAME).is_file():\n            return d / FILENAME\n        if (d / \"parts\").is_dir():\n            return None\n    return None\n\n\ndef _read(path):\n    \"\"\"Parse it, and say which file when it will not parse. The same courtesy cards get.\"\"\"\n    try:\n        return tomllib.loads(path.read_text(encoding=\"utf-8\"))\n    except tomllib.TOMLDecodeError as exc:\n        raise MeasurementError(f\"{path.name}: not valid TOML ({exc})\") from exc\n\n\ndef load(start=None):\n    \"\"\"Every measurement on file, as name -> record. Empty when there is no file.\"\"\"\n    path = _find(pathlib.Path(start).resolve() if start else _caller_dir(2))\n    return _read(path) if path else {}\n\n\ndef provisional(start=None):\n    \"\"\"Every value on file that admits it was not actually measured.\n\n    The doctrine says to ask rather than improvise, and sometimes there is nobody to\n    ask: the caliper is in a drawer, the object is at the office, the print has to go\n    on tonight. The failure mode is not putting a number in, it is that the number then\n    looks exactly like one somebody measured. Six months later nothing distinguishes\n    them and the part is wrong for a reason no one can find.\n\n    So a guess is allowed to be written down and required to say so, and `nurb check`\n    reports the ones that still are. `how` stays mandatory either way: a provisional\n    value still has to say where it came from, because \"eyeballed against a broom\"\n    tells the next person how much to trust it.\n    \"\"\"\n    return sorted(\n        (name, entry.get(\"how\", \"\"))\n        for name, entry in load(start).items()\n        if entry.get(\"provisional\")\n    )\n\n\ndef measured(name, start=None):\n    \"\"\"A measured value, in mm.\n\n    Raises when it is not on file, when it has no provenance, or when it is recorded in\n    units this does not work in. All three are cases where continuing would produce\n    geometry that looks right and is wrong.\n\n    A value marked `provisional = true` is returned like any other. It is a real number\n    that builds a real part; what it is not is measured, and `nurb check` says so until\n    somebody picks up a caliper.\n    \"\"\"\n    start = pathlib.Path(start).resolve() if start else _caller_dir(2)\n    path = _find(start)\n    if path is None:\n        raise MeasurementError(\n            f\"no {FILENAME} above {start}, so there is nothing measured to ask for. \"\n            f\"Measure {name!r}, then record it:\\n\\n\"\n            f'  [{name}]\\n  value = 0\\n  unit = \"mm\"\\n  how = \"how you measured it\"\\n\\n'\n            f\"If there is nobody to ask right now, add `provisional = true` and put \"\n            f\"your best guess in. `nurb check` will keep reminding you.\"\n        )\n    book = _read(path)\n    if name not in book:\n        have = \", \".join(sorted(book)) or \"nothing yet\"\n        raise MeasurementError(\n            f\"nothing measured called {name!r} in {path.name}. have: {have}. \"\n            f\"Ask for the measurement rather than guessing one: a wrong number here \"\n            f\"builds, checks clean, and prints.\"\n        )\n    entry = book[name]\n    if \"value\" not in entry:\n        raise MeasurementError(f\"{path.name}: {name} has no value\")\n    if \"how\" not in entry:\n        raise MeasurementError(\n            f\"{path.name}: {name} does not say how it was measured. A value without \"\n            f\"provenance is a guess with a filename on it.\"\n        )\n    unit = entry.get(\"unit\", \"mm\")\n    if unit != \"mm\":\n        raise MeasurementError(f\"{path.name}: {name} is in {unit!r}, and nurb works in mm\")\n    return entry[\"value\"]","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}

A count above the baseline is a regression; at or below it is\n    silence. The count is the assertion, not the individual faces, because which face\n    is which shifts as soon as anything upstream of the polish pass moves.\n    \"\"\"\n    small = [f for f in shape.faces() if f.area < ctx.sliver_area]\n    allowed = ctx.accepted.get(\"sliver\", 0)\n    if len(small) <= allowed:\n        return []\n    worst = min(small, key=lambda f: f.area)\n    return [\n        Finding(\n            \"sliver\",\n            WARN,\n            f\"{len(small)} faces under {ctx.sliver_area}mm2, {allowed} accounted for\",\n            plain=f\"{len(small) - allowed} detail{'' if len(small) - allowed == 1 else 's'} \"\n            \"too small to print cleanly, coming out as smears rather than shapes\",\n            value=round(worst.area, 3),\n            where=tuple(round(v, 2) for v in worst.center()),\n        )\n    ]\n\n\n@rule(\"build_volume\")\ndef build_volume(shape, ctx):\n    \"\"\"Does it fit on the printer at all.\"\"\"\n    size = shape.bounding_box().size\n    up = Vector(*ctx.up).normalized()\n    if _standing(shape, up) is None:\n        got = sorted([size.X, size.Y, size.Z], reverse=True)\n        fits = all(g <= b for g, b in zip(got, sorted(ctx.bed, reverse=True)))\n        if not fits:\n            # The box is the wrong question for a part that fits turned on the\n            # plate: issue #55's 364mm tray sits on a 350mm bed at 36 degrees.\n            fits = _fits_rotated(shape, ctx.bed)\n        orientation = \"in any orientation\"\n    else:\n        # A stance facet fixes the build direction. The part may turn on the plate,\n        # but rolling it onto another axis would put the facet in the air again.\n        bed, top = _span(shape, up)\n        height = top - bed\n        footprint = sorted(\n            _span(shape, axis)[1] - _span(shape, axis)[0]\n            for axis in _bed_axes(up)\n        )\n        plate = sorted(ctx.bed[:2])\n        got = [height, *footprint]\n        fits = height <= ctx.bed[2] and (\n            all(got <= available for got, available in zip(footprint, plate))\n            or _fits_rotated(shape, ctx.bed, up)\n        )\n        orientation = \"in its fixed build orientation\"\n    if fits:\n        return []\n    return [\n        Finding(\n            \"build_volume\",\n            FAIL,\n            f\"{size.X:.0f} x {size.Y:.0f} x {size.Z:.0f}mm does not fit \"\n            f\"{ctx.bed[0]:.0f} x {ctx.bed[1]:.0f} x {ctx.bed[2]:.0f}mm {orientation}\",\n            value=round(max(got), 1),\n        )\n    ]\n\n\ndef _sample_normals(face, grid=5):\n    \"\"\"Outward normals across a face.\n\n    A planar face has one and it is exact. A curved face does not, and the sampling\n    has to reach the ends of its parameter range: sampling cell midpoints instead\n    walks a cylinder round at 45, 135, 225 and 315 degrees and never once looks\n    straight down, which is the only normal an overhang check cares about. Endpoints\n    included, the same grid hits 270 exactly.\n\n=== bed_bevel ===\ndef bed_bevel(shape, ctx):\n    \"\"\"Polish laid on the edges that meet the build plate.\n\n    A chamfer down there buys nothing and costs a mess: it turns the first layer into\n    a knife edge that squashes, and on a mount-facing part it stops the thing sitting\n    flat.\n\n    A tilted face touching the bed is not always a bevel, which this rule assumed and\n    the calipers holder disproved. The doctrine's corbel is a 45 degree underside, and\n    where it lands on the plate rather than dying into the body it is exactly such a\n    face: structure, prescribed, and 46mm2 of it.\n\n    What separates them is how far the face rises off the bed, and a bottom chamfer's\n    rise is its size exactly. Measured: 1.00, 2.00 and 3.00mm for chamfers of those\n    sizes, against 4.29mm for the corbel. Rise is independent of the edge's path: a 1mm\n    chamfer around a 20mm cylinder is still 1mm high even though its plan-view bounding\n    box is 20mm in both directions. The limit is three times the polish size because the\n    largest chamfer this doctrine puts anywhere is the 3mm structural relief, and the\n    smallest corbel starts with a 4 to 6mm vertical tip.\n    \"\"\"\n    up = Vector(*ctx.up).normalized()\n    bed, _ = _span(shape, up)\n    if _standing(shape, up) is not None:\n        # Standing on a facet, so every face at the plate is deliberately tilted and\n        # there is no bottom whose rim a bevel could dress. A 4mm wall stood at 45\n        # rises 2.83mm, square in the chamfer band, and prints fine: rise cannot tell\n        # it from a bevel, but the stance can.\n        return []\n    found = []\n    for face in shape.faces():\n        low, high = _span(face, up)\n        if low > bed + 1e-4:\n            continue  # does not reach the plate\n        if high - low > 3 * ctx.cosmetic_chamfer:\n            continue  # rises too far to be a chamfer band, so it is structure\n        for normal in _sample_normals(face):\n            tilt = abs(normal.dot(up))\n            if 0.03 < tilt < 0.97:  # neither lying on the plate nor standing on it\n                found.append(\n                    Finding(\n                        \"bed_bevel\",\n                        WARN,\n                        f\"{degrees(asin(min(1.0, tilt))):.0f}deg face meets the build \"\n                        f\"plate over {face.area:.1f}mm2, which is polish on the first layer\",\n                        plain=f\"a chamfer along {face.area:.0f}mm2 of the bottom edge, \"\n                        \"where it costs the first layer its grip on the plate\",\n                        value=round(face.area, 2),\n                        where=tuple(round(v, 2) for v in face.center()),\n                    )\n                )\n                break\n    return found\n\n\n@rule(\"concave_cosmetic\")\ndef concave_cosmetic(shape, ctx):\n    \"\"\"Polish laid into an inside corner instead of across an outside one.\n\n    A cosmetic chamfer belongs on a convex edge, where it takes a sharp corner off. Run\n    over a concave one it does the opposite: it adds a thin wedge into the corner,\n    which prints as a feather edge and collects stress where the part is already\n    weakest. A concave junction that needs relieving wants a deliberate structural\n    chamfer sized for the load, not the polish pass.","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"96cf8d31-4e94-4d55-a648-73c13c46b367","uuid":"fe38f245-a6f8-4df7-a2ba-34ad03f052c6","timestamp":"2026-08-24T23:33:38.866Z","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"def sliver(shape, ctx):\n    \"\"\"Faces too small to print as anything but a smear.\n\n    Chamfers meeting at a corner legitimately make a few, so a part declares how many\n    it has earned. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Tc6pXK6iMWQeJtKip35Xo1","type":"tool_result","content":"16:[bambu_p1s]\n17-bed = [256.0, 256.0, 256.0]\n18-slicer = \"Bambu Lab P1S\"\n19-\n20-[bambu_p1p]\n21-bed = [256.0, 256.0, 256.0]\n22-slicer = \"Bambu Lab P1P\"\n23-\n24-[bambu_a1]\n25-bed = [256.0, 256.0, 256.0]\n26-slicer = \"Bambu Lab A1\"\n27-\n28-[bambu_a1_mini]\n=== polish ===\n\"\"\"The polish pass: chamfer as much of a set as the kernel will take.\n\n`chamfer` is all or nothing. One edge that cannot land takes the whole call down, and\nthe natural response is to shrink the set by hand until it builds. That is how a part\nwith ninety exposed edges ends up with three chamfered ones, and it is why the same\ndoctrine produces far more chamfers in a GUI, where a human adds them one feature at a\ntime and works around each collision individually.\n\nThe doctrine describes this algorithm and used to leave every project to write it. It\nwas 35 lines in one `system.py`, which meant the next project would write it again or,\nfar more likely, skip it and hand-narrow the set, which is exactly what the rule exists\nto prevent.\n\"\"\"\n\nimport collections.abc\nimport functools\n\nfrom build123d import chamfer as _chamfer\n\n# What OCCT says when a chamfer will not land. The same strings `nurb verify`\n# matches on, because both are asking the same question: did the kernel refuse, or did\n# the part refuse in its own words.\nKERNEL = (\"Failed creating a chamfer\", \"BRep_API\")\n\n\ndef _advice(count, size, word):\n    \"\"\"What the doctrine knows about this failure, said at the moment it happens.\n\n    The kernel's own message is \"try a smaller length value(s)\", and the doctrine is\n    explicit that following it would never have found the real rule: a chamfer fails\n    for room, not for length. Shrinking it is the one response that makes a part quietly\n    worse, because a 0.4mm chamfer lands and then reads as a defect on the print.\n\n    So the rule goes here rather than only in `nurb rules`. This is the most common way\n    a part stops building, and an error is the one place a reader is guaranteed to be\n    looking.\n    \"\"\"\n    lines = [\n        \"\",\n        f\"  A {word} fails for room, not for length, and shrinking it is usually the\",\n        f\"  wrong fix. Two {word}ed convex edges need more than {2 * size:g}mm of face between\",\n        f\"  them, and a single one needs more than {size:g}mm.\",\n    ]\n    if count > 1:\n        lines += [\n            \"\",\n            f\"  This call passed {count} edges as one batch, and a batch is all or nothing:\",\n            \"  one edge that cannot land takes the whole call down. Every edge in it very\",\n            \"  likely chamfers fine on its own, so testing them one at a time will report\",\n            \"  that nothing is wrong.\",\n            \"\",\n            # `!r` not `:g`: a size is a measurement, and this codebase reads a bare `1`\n            # as a count. One edge that cannot land takes the whole call down, and\nthe natural response is to shrink the set by hand until it builds. That is how a part\nwith ninety exposed edges ends up with three chamfered ones, and it is why the same\ndoctrine produces far more chamfers in a GUI, where a human adds them one feature at a\ntime and works around each collision individually.\n\nThe doctrine describes this algorithm and used to leave every project to write it. It\nwas 35 lines in one `system.py`, which meant the next project would write it again or,\nfar more likely, skip it and hand-narrow the set, which is exactly what the rule exists\nto prevent.\n\"\"\"\n\nimport collections.abc\nimport functools\n\nfrom build123d import chamfer as _chamfer\n\n# What OCCT says when a chamfer will not land. The same strings `nurb verify`\n# matches on, because both are asking the same question: did the kernel refuse, or did\n# the part refuse in its own words.\nKERNEL = (\"Failed creating a chamfer\", \"BRep_API\")\n\n\ndef _advice(count, size, word):\n    \"\"\"What the doctrine knows about this failure, said at the moment it happens.\n\n    The kernel's own message is \"try a smaller length value(s)\", and the doctrine is\n    explicit that following it would never have found the real rule: a chamfer fails\n    for room, not for length. Shrinking it is the one response that makes a part quietly\n    worse, because a 0.4mm chamfer lands and then reads as a defect on the print.\n\n    So the rule goes here rather than only in `nurb rules`. This is the most common way\n    a part stops building, and an error is the one place a reader is guaranteed to be\n    looking.\n    \"\"\"\n    lines = [\n        \"\",\n        f\"  A {word} fails for room, not for length, and shrinking it is usually the\",\n        f\"  wrong fix. Two {word}ed convex edges need more than {2 * size:g}mm of face between\",\n        f\"  them, and a single one needs more than {size:g}mm.\",\n    ]\n    if count > 1:\n        lines += [\n            \"\",\n            f\"  This call passed {count} edges as one batch, and a batch is all or nothing:\",\n            \"  one edge that cannot land takes the whole call down. Every edge in it very\",\n            \"  likely chamfers fine on its own, so testing them one at a time will report\",\n            \"  that nothing is wrong.\",\n            \"\",\n            # `!r` not `:g`: a size is a measurement, and this codebase reads a bare `1`\n            # as a count. Chamfer in two passes and reselect from the result.\",\n        ]\n    lines += [\"\", \"  `nurb rules` has both cases in full.\"]\n    return \"\\n\".join(lines)\n\n\ndef _count(objects):\n    try:\n        return len(list(objects))\n    except TypeError:  # a single Edge or Vertex, which is not iterable\n        return 1\n\n\ndef _guarded(fn, word, objects, size, *args, **kwargs):\n    # A generator would be exhausted by the failing call, so the count in the message\n    # would read zero and the advice would describe the wrong case. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_014VQ4D98DRoHYeuAjnsPCuA","type":"tool_result","content":"## Polish pass\n\nThe polish pass runs last, after structure is finished.\n\n1. **Chamfers are the default on exposed edges**, 1mm, never below 0.8mm. A consistent faceted look that prints reliably beats a fillet default. The one sanctioned round-edge treatment is `crown`, for the top rim of a closed perimeter wall, and it is **asked for, never assumed**: reach for it when the user wants a rounded rim, or when a mating part genuinely requires one, and chamfer the rim like any other edge otherwise. It exists because filleting a variable-height roofline directly dies in OCCT's corner capping, not because rims want beads.\n2. **No chamfers lying in the back face or the bottom face.** The back sits against the\n   wall and the bottom is the bed-contact face. Chamfering an edge that lies in either\n   buys nothing, and where a bottom chamfer meets another one it makes sliver facets and\n   notch points that are very hard to print.\n   **An edge that merely ends there is fine**, and this is the distinction to get right,\n   because reading the rule as \"nothing touching the bed\" throws away the vertical corner\n   chamfers, which are the most-handled edges on the part. A vertical corner's chamfer\n   stands square to the plate and the first layer keeps its full width. A *sloped* edge\n   arriving at the plate is the case that is not fine: its chamfer lands tilted and lays\n   a knife edge into the first layer. That is exactly what `bed_bevel` measures, and it\n   tells a corbel from a chamfer by reach, since a chamfer's reach is its size.\n3. **Never touch fit-critical mating geometry**: channels, dovetails, sockets, anything\n   that has to slide onto something else. Lead-in chamfers at a mating mouth sound\n   helpful and are not. They make tiny compound facets that print badly on someone\n   else's machine.\n4. **Never polish a concave edge.** A cosmetic chamfer on an inside corner adds a thin\n   wedge instead of taking a corner off: it prints as a feather edge and collects stress\n   where the part is already weakest. A concave junction that needs relieving gets a\n   deliberate 3mm structural chamfer, sized for the load, before the polish pass runs.\n   `is_convex` and `concave_edges` are in the vocabulary a part file gets, because this\n   is the one polish mistake that is invisible in code: the selector reads as an\n   ordinary exposed-edge query and the part builds, exports and prints without\n   complaint. It shipped in this library once.\n5. **Select chamfer edges by filtering, never blanket-chamfer.** Mating edges, back-face\n   edges, bottom-face edges and concave edges all have to be excluded.\n   **Filter for what must not be touched, then let the kernel refuse the rest.** A\n   chamfer call is all or nothing, so one edge that cannot land takes the whole pass\n   down, and the tempting response is to keep narrowing the set by hand until it builds.\n   That is how a part ends up with three chamfered edges out of ninety. Narrow the set\n   for reasons you can name, then chamfer greedily: try the set, and where it fails,\n   bisect and keep the halves that build. Refuse any batch that makes a face smaller\n   than the corner triangle three chamfers leave, about `0.866 * size ** 2`, since\n   anything smaller is chamfers colliding. Chamfer the original solid with the whole\n   accepted set each time, never the result of the last attempt: an edge belongs to the\n   shape it was selected from, and applying batches in sequence quietly re-chamfers the\n   first body and returns it.\n6. **No text labels on parts.** File naming carries catalog identity. There is a second\n   reason beyond taste: a glyph's outline comes from a system font, so a part with text\n   on it builds to different geometry on a different machine. The calibration coupon,\n   which needs a label because four of them come off one plate looking identical, comes\n   out at 2600.6mm3 with 88 faces on one machine and 2601.0 with 83 on another. Nothing\n   about its fit moves, but its card cannot be asserted anywhere but where it was\n   written.\n7. **Consistency is the polish.** One chamfer size across a family is what makes it read\n   as a designed system rather than a pile of prints.\n\n**Sliver check after every polish pass.** Faces under 1mm² are a polish bug unless\njustified. The only ones the doctrine allows outright are the corner triangles where\nthree 1mm chamfers meet at a convex corner, about 0.87mm². Anything else is usually\nchamfers colliding near a mating mouth, or a chamfered concave edge. A part declares the\ncount it has earned on its card, so a new one is a regression and a known one is silent.\n\n## Kernel rules\n\nSpecific to OCCT, and measured on real parts rather than reasoned about.\n\n- **Two chamfered convex edges need more than `2 * chamfer_size` of face between them.**\n  Closer and OCCT raises `BRep_API: command not done`, which build123d surfaces as\n  \"Failed creating a chamfer, try a smaller length value(s)\". At 1mm the shelf fails\n  with 1.66mm between the edges and builds with 2.16mm; at 0.5mm it builds with 1.16mm.\n  The threshold tracks the chamfer exactly. This is the single most common way a part\n  stops building, so `chamfer` says this much at the point of failure rather than\n  leaving the kernel's advice to stand: taking \"try a smaller length\" at its word is how\n  a part ends up with a 0.4mm chamfer that lands and then prints as a defect.\n- **One chamfered edge needs more than `chamfer_size` of face**, which is the same rule\n  with one chamfer instead of two and is easy to miss because it looks like plenty of\n  room. It bites wherever a polished edge sits beside something that is never polished:\n  a concave junction, a structural chamfer's toe, a pocket wall. Measured on three\n  different parts, all at the same threshold: 1.08mm builds and 1.00mm fails at a 1mm\n  chamfer.\n- **A batch chamfer failure is not a bad edge.** Every edge in a failing set chamfers\n  fine on its own, so testing them one at a time reports that nothing is wrong. Bisect\n  the set pairwise instead. \"Try a smaller length\" would never have found the rule above.\n- **The second way a chamfer dies, where bisecting does not help**, is an edge whose end\n  lands on a vertex with four faces around it and only three edges between them. Two of\n  those faces touch at a point without sharing an edge, and OCCT has no cap for that\n  corner. The edge fails on its own, on a clean body, at every length down to 0.05mm, so\n  nothing about the failure looks like a clearance problem. The fix is to chamfer in two\n  passes so that the first one gives those two faces a real edge to meet along, then\n  reselect from the result. Found on the parts bin, where the front drop and the side\n  taper land at the same height by design.\n- **Prefer `new_edges` over geometric selectors.** Each chamfer changes topology, so a\n  selector resolved against pristine geometry drifts once an earlier chamfer runs.\n  `new_edges(before, combined=after)` returns exactly the edges an operation created. It\n  is the algebra-mode equivalent of a builder's `Select.LAST`, and algebra mode is what a\n  part file uses, so `part.edges() - last` is not available. It is what makes a part\n  survive flexing its counts.\n- **A loft through three or more sections wanders unless `ruled=True`.** With two sections the flag changes nothing, measured to the same volume, so a plain taper is fine either way. At three, the smooth default fits one spline through all of them and the wall between sections is nowhere the numbers say: lofting a 20mm square through a 10mm waist, the wall halfway there reads 12.5mm across where the straight line says 15. Every wall thickness and overhang worked out from the section dimensions drifts by that much, silently, between the stations. `ruled=True` puts the wall exactly on the straight line between sections, which is why the gridfinity shelf builds as one five-section ruled loft. Mismatched sections are not the hazard they look like: a square lofts to a hexagon or to its own 45 degree twin without complaint, so there is no vertex-matching rule, only the spline one.\n- **A ruled loft between polygons polishes like a prism; a smooth loft to a curve does not.** The rim where a square meets a circle refuses a 1mm chamfer and accepts 0.5, and taking the kernel's \"smaller length\" advice there walks straight under the 0.8mm floor. When a lofted part is going to be polished, prefer ruled polygonal sections; a rim that has to be round anyway is `crown`'s case, not a shrinking chamfer's.\n- **Loft does not police its sections.** Two sections landing nearly in one plane, the shape a `Pos` typo makes, return a fraction-of-a-mm3 sliver solid instead of raising. It builds, exports and passes the one-solid check, so the bounding box and the render are what catch it. A loft whose result looks wrong by eye is wrong at a section, not at the surface.\n- **A Fusion workaround is sometimes a real constraint wearing a disguise.** The gusset\n  drop that existed only to escape `ASM_BL_NO_MATE` turned out to be the same \"a chamfer\n  needs room to land\" constraint at a different number. Deleting it would have been\n  wrong; restating it as a rule was right.\n\n## Cards\n\nA part file carries geometry, not rationale. Every part worth keeping has a card next to\nit, same basename. **Read the card before editing the part, and update it when done.**\n\nFour sections, and one of them does work nothing else does:","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"96cf8d31-4e94-4d55-a648-73c13c46b367","uuid":"08475094-7b97-4b1e-9873-01ae109ed0b4","timestamp":"2026-08-24T23:34:23.437Z","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"## Polish pass\n\nThe polish pass runs last, after structure is finished.\n\n1. **Chamfers are the default on exposed edges**, 1mm, never below 0.8mm. Every wall thickness and overhang worked out from the section dimensions drifts by that much, silently, between the stations. `ruled=True` puts the wall exactly on the straight line between sections, which is why the gridfinity shelf builds as one five-section ruled loft. Mismatched sections are not the hazard they look like: a square lofts to a hexagon or to its own 45 degree twin without complaint, so there is no vertex-matching rule, only the spline one.\n- **A ruled loft between polygons polishes like a prism; a smooth loft to a curve does not.** The rim where a square meets a circle refuses a 1mm chamfer and accepts 0.5, and taking the kernel's \"smaller length\" advice there walks straight under the 0.8mm floor. When a lofted part is going to be polished, prefer ruled polygonal sections; a rim that has to be round anyway is `crown`'s case, not a shrinking chamfer's.\n- **Loft does not police its sections.** Two sections landing nearly in one plane, the shape a `Pos` typo makes, return a fraction-of-a-mm3 sliver solid instead of raising. It builds, exports and passes the one-solid check, so the bounding box and the render are what catch it. A loft whose result looks wrong by eye is wrong at a section, not at the surface.\n- **A Fusion workaround is sometimes a real constraint wearing a disguise.** The gusset\n  drop that existed only to escape `ASM_BL_NO_MATE` turned out to be the same \"a chamfer\n  needs room to land\" constraint at a different number. Deleting it would have been\n  wrong; restating it as a rule was right.\n\n## Cards\n\nA part file carries geometry, not rationale. Every part worth keeping has a card next to\nit, same basename. **Read the card before editing the part, and update it when done.**\n\nFour sections, and one of them does work nothing else does:","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}

Every part worth keeping has a card next to\nit, same basename. **Read the card before editing the part, and update it when done.**\n\nFour sections, and one of them does work nothing else does:\n\n- `## What it is`\n- `## Design notes`, why the key dimensions are what they are\n- `## Don't`, **required**, what was tried and rejected. Geometry records what is; only\n  this records what is deliberately absent. Without it an agent helpfully re-adds the\n  lead-in chamfer that was retired on purpose.\n- `## Changelog`, dated\n\nPlus two machine-facing pieces:\n\n- The **AUTO block**, regenerated by `nurb card`. Never hand-edit it. It is a cache, not\n  a source of truth, and it holds no timestamp so regenerating it on unchanged geometry\n  produces no diff.\n- The **accepted baselines**, a TOML fence carrying what this part has already justified:\n  its sliver count, its real minimum wall, its printer settings. The number goes next to\n  the sentence that earns it, because a count on its own is a magic number. Machine facts\n  stay out of it: a bed size belongs to the machine, so it lives in `printer.toml` at the\n  project root, which names a shipped profile (`profile = \"bambu_a1_mini\"`) and can\n  override any check setting machine-wide. A printer is really a fact about the workshop,\n  not the project, so `~/.config/nurb/config.toml` takes the same schema and covers every\n  project on the machine; `printer.toml` overrides it where they disagree.\n\n```toml\n[part]\nmin_wall = 1.0\nforward = [-1, 0, 0]\n\n[accepted]\nsliver = 6\n```\n\n### Variants\n\nA catalog entry that is the same function at different numbers is a variant, not a new\nfile. It goes in the same fence, and `build`, `check`, `card` and `export` all walk it\nexactly as they walk the part's own defaults, so it gets its own STL, its own baselines\nand its own line in the AUTO block.\n\n```toml\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2]\nnote = \"The same shelf sized for the wide bin: three columns instead of two.\"\n\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2.params]\ngrid_x = 3\nbracket_count = 6\n\n[variants.shelf_gridfinity_3x2.accepted]\nsliver = 26\n```\n\nThe test is whether it is the same geometry. A wider cradle on the same J is a variant.\nA different mechanism is a part, however similar the slab looks.\n\n`note` is one sentence saying why the variant exists, written in the user's words rather than the doctrine's, because the viewer shows it next to the variant and its reader is whoever is about to print the thing. The params are the how and never the why: `diagonal = true` explains nothing to someone who has not read the doctrine, and \"prints tilted so it needs no supports\" does.\n\n## Measurements\n## Verification\n\n\"It built\" is not verification. `nurb verify` runs the machine-checkable part of\nthis list: one solid per configuration, every count flexed upward, the rules clean,\nand the card agreeing with the geometry. `--report` writes the verdict into\n`build/renders/<part>.verify.md` with the renders that back it beside it, one still\nper finding. The two it cannot do are the two that need you, and they are items 2 and 6.\n\nBefore presenting a part:\n\n1. **Flex the driving parameters up as well as down**, for example 4 to 6 to 2 to 4.\n   Growth is what catches a frozen selector; shrinking alone passes a broken part.\n2. **Check fit-critical faces by coordinate** after any polish or resize. `nurb inspect`\n   lists them, with each finding resolved to the face it fired on. Which faces are\n   fit-critical is still yours to know, which is why this is not something `nurb verify`\n   can run for you.\n3. **Confirm the solid count**, which is almost always one.\n4. **Run `nurb check`.** Zero findings is the bar, and a finding fired at a part that\n   prints fine is a bug in the rule, not something to accept quietly.\n5. **Predict a baseline before you look at it.** Working out what the sliver count\n   should be from the polish exclusions turns the number into a test of the rule instead\n   of something to write down.\n6. **Render it and actually look.** `nurb render <part>` writes a PNG, `--section\n   z:4mm` cuts it open where outside views cannot reach, and `nurb inspect --render`\n   stands a camera at every finding with the guilty face painted. {"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-opus-5","id":"msg_011CeNT3sKUyEBXFC216aKEu","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_01QV7rdeZfxYP4Q4zBGdBStX","name":"Bash","input":{"command":"cat > parts/leg_cup.py <<'EOF'\nfrom nurb import *\n\n\n@part\ndef leg_cup(\n    lift=measured(\"lift\"),\n    pocket_depth=8.0,\n    wall_thickness=2.0,\n    leg_slack=0.4,\n    corner_chamfer=1.0,\n    draft=False,\n):\n    \"\"\"A cup the short bench leg drops into, lifting it until the bench stops rocking.\n\n    lift: how far the cup raises the leg off the floor, the solid floor under the foot\n    pocket_depth: how deep the leg sits in the cup before it bottoms out\n    wall_thickness: how thick the four walls around the leg are\n    leg_slack: how much wider than the leg the pocket is, so the foot drops in\n    corner_chamfer: how much is taken off the four outside corners\n    \"\"\"\n    leg_width = measured(\"leg_width\")\n    leg_depth = measured(\"leg_depth\")\n\n    if lift <= 0:\n        reject(\n            f\"lift {lift:g} leaves no floor under the foot, so the cup would be a \"\n            f\"bottomless sleeve: raise it above 0\",\n            param=\"lift\",\n        )\n\n    pocket_width = leg_width + leg_slack\n    pocket_length = leg_depth + leg_slack\n    outer_width = pocket_width + 2 * wall_thickness\n    outer_length = pocket_length + 2 * wall_thickness\n\n    up_from_bed = (Align.CENTER, Align.CENTER, Align.MIN)\n    body = Box(outer_width, outer_length, lift + pocket_depth, align=up_from_bed)\n    # The cut runs past the rim rather than stopping flush on it: a coincident face is\n    # work the kernel does not need, and the pocket is still exactly `pocket_depth`\n    # deep because its floor sits at `lift` and the rim is where the body ends.\n    pocket = Pos(0, 0, lift) * Box(\n        pocket_width, pocket_length, pocket_depth + 1.0, align=up_from_bed\n    )\n    cup = body - pocket\n\n    if draft:\n        return cup\n    # Only the four outside corners. The rim stays square, because a chamfer there\n    # eats the wall the leg leans on and a lead-in inside the pocket is polish laid\n    # on mating geometry; a vertical corner's chamfer stands square to the plate and\n    # costs the first layer nothing.\n    inner_reach = max(pocket_width, pocket_length) / 2\n    corners = cup.edges().filter_by(Axis.Z).filter_by(\n        lambda e: abs(e.bounding_box().center().X) > inner_reach\n        or abs(e.bounding_box().center().Y) > inner_reach\n    )\n    return polish(cup, corners, corner_chamfer)\nEOF\nnurb build leg_cup 2>&1 | tail -20"},"caller":{"type":"direct"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"stop_details":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":2,"cache_creation_input_tokens":2513,"cache_read_input_tokens":73295,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":0,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":2513},"output_tokens":17,"service_tier":"standard","inference_geo":"not_available"},"diagnostics":null,"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"96cf8d31-4e94-4d55-a648-73c13c46b367","uuid":"29e9b40f-0903-45dc-8734-8ec78cd45344","timestamp":"2026-08-24T23:35:14.585Z","request_id":"req_011CeNT3rQQwBQVWGWb7JKox"}

{"type":"assistant","message":{"model":"claude-opus-5","id":"msg_011CeNT9DKeDBtujWfZ7DUwZ","type":"message","role":"assistant","content":[{"type":"tool_use","id":"toolu_01SbPdstNsCUGEyemW2kE1M1","name":"Bash","input":{"command":"cat > parts/leg_cup.md <<'EOF'\n# leg_cup\n\n## What it is\n\nA slip-over foot cup for the short leg of the workbench. It sits on the floor, the\nleg's foot drops into the pocket from above, and the solid floor under the foot lifts\nthat corner until the bench stops rocking. One cup, one leg; the other three legs keep\nstanding on the floor.\n\n## Design notes\n\n- The pocket is 22.4 x 18.9mm: the leg measured 22.0 x 18.5 with calipers, plus 0.4 of\n  total slack across each dimension so the foot drops in rather than being driven in.\n  That is between the fits table's snug (0.2) and free (0.5), which is what a foot that\n  has to go on by hand and then stay put wants.\n- **`lift` is provisional.** Nobody could get to the bench to measure the wobble, and\n  the shim that fell out read somewhere between 2 and 5mm by eye, so `lift` is on file\n  at 3.5, the middle of that range, marked `provisional = true`. `nurb check` will keep\n  saying so until somebody puts a caliper on it. Everything is derived from it, so\n  correcting the number in `measurements.toml` and rebuilding is the whole fix, and the\n  slider answers the same question live: overall height is `lift + 8`, and nothing else\n  moves.\n- Walls are 2.0mm, the doctrine's minimum, all the way to the rim. This part carries\n  the bench's weight in pure compression down the walls and the floor, which is the one\n  load FDM is good at, so there is nothing here to gusset or thicken.\n- The pocket is 8mm deep, about a third of the leg's width. Deep enough that the cup\n  cannot rock off the foot or tip when the bench is dragged, shallow enough that the\n  whole part prints in a couple of minutes.\n- Prints as it sits: flat bottom on the bed, pocket up, one solid, no supports. The\n  pocket is an open vertical prism, which is self-supporting by definition, and the\n  600mm2 first layer is nowhere near warp territory.\n\n## Don't\n\n- **Don't chamfer the rim, inside or out.** The wall is 2.0mm, the doctrine's floor, so\n  a 1mm chamfer on either top edge takes half of it and two of them meet in a knife\n  edge. A rim chamfer also stops the wall being 2.0 where the leg leans hardest.\n- **Don't add a lead-in chamfer at the pocket mouth.** It sounds helpful on a part\n  something drops into and it is polish laid on mating geometry: it widens the mouth\n  the fit was measured for and prints as compound facets that vary machine to machine.\n  The 0.4 of slack is what makes the foot drop in.\n- **Don't chamfer the bottom rim.** It lands tilted in the first layer, which is what\n  `bed_bevel` measures, and this part's whole job is sitting flat on the floor. The\n  four vertical corner chamfers reach the plate and are fine: they stand square to it.\n- **Don't bake the lift into the part file.** It is the one number nobody has measured;\n  it lives in `measurements.toml` so that the correction is a value, not an edit.\n- **Don't drain or vent the floor.** A hole under the foot is a hole in the only thing\n  holding the bench up, and there is nothing in there to drain.\n- **Don't print this in TPU** because it is a foot. A squashy cup gives the wobble back.\n\n## Changelog\n\n- 2026-08-24: First version. Pocket from the calipered leg section, lift provisional at\n  3.5mm pending a measurement at the shop.\nEOF\nnurb card leg_cup 2>&1 | tail -5; echo ===; cat parts/leg_cup.md | tail -25"},"caller":{"type":"direct"}}],"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"stop_details":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":2,"cache_creation_input_tokens":951,"cache_read_input_tokens":81499,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":0,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":951},"output_tokens":3,"service_tier":"standard","inference_geo":"not_available"},"diagnostics":null,"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"96cf8d31-4e94-4d55-a648-73c13c46b367","uuid":"7b476b66-acd9-4a49-aa79-4ffaaa389562","timestamp":"2026-08-24T23:36:36.752Z","request_id":"req_011CeNT9CRKjwMUcvKxn5abU"}
